```sql
SELECT
    EXTRACT(YEAR FROM o.o_orderdate) AS o_year,
    COALESCE(
        SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount))
            FILTER (WHERE n2.n_name = 'CANADA'),
        0
    ) / SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS mkt_share
FROM part p
JOIN lineitem l ON l.l_partkey = p.p</produ_partkey
JOIN supplier sced_sql>

After a careful line-by-line comparison of the three EXPLAIN plans (original, rewritten, and enhanced), all three plans are structurally and numerically identical. The only difference is the use of table aliases in the rewritten and enhanced plans (e.g., `orders` → `o`, `lineitem` → `l`, `supplier` → `s`, `customer` → `c`, `part` → `p`, `region` → `r`), which affects only the display of relation names, not the query plan itself. Every cost value, row estimate, width, scan type, join algorithm, parallel worker setting, and plan-node hierarchy is unchanged across all three versions.

1. Cost Efficiency:
   - Overall cost change percentage: 0%. The total plan cost remains 1917045.25..1917965.35 in all three plans. The rewritten and enhanced queries produce no planner-level cost difference.
   - Cost change at the most expensive plan node: 0%. The dominant cost node, the Parallel Seq Scan on `lineitem`/`l` (cost 0.00..1375081.30, ~72% of total plan cost), is identical in all plans. The second-largest contributor, the inner Parallel Hash Join over lineitem-part (cost 51477.88..1492167.85), is also unchanged.

2. Plan Characteristics:
   - Scan type transitions: None. All three plans use Parallel Seq Scan on the large fact tables (lineitem, orders, customer, part, supplier) and Seq Scan on the small dimension tables (nation, region). No index scans or bitmap scans were introduced. This is expected since the rewrite only altered aliases, and no new indexes or predicate rewrites (e.g., indexable date-range expressions) were applied.
   - Join algorithm refinements: None. The join tree remains identical: a chain of Parallel Hash Joins (supplier⋈lineitem, orders⋈lineitem, orders⋈customer, lineitem⋈part) wrapped by a non-parallel Hash Join on supplier⋈nation, with small Hash Joins for nation/region lookups. No transition to Merge Join or Nested Loop occurred.
   - Elimination of explicit sorting and reduction of intermediate result sets: None. The explicit Sort node before Partial GroupAggregate (cost 1916045.24..1916080.65, sort key EXTRACT(year FROM o_orderdate)) persists in all plans. Row estimates at every node (e.g., 14161 rows after the final join, 2406 groups) are unchanged, so intermediate result set sizes are identical.

3. Resource Utilization:
   - Memory usage: Unchanged. All Hash and Parallel Hash nodes build with identical estimated rows and widths (e.g., Parallel Hash for lineitem-side join at 166071 rows × width 20; customer hash at 125000 rows × width 4), so hash-table memory consumption is identical.

Need include "TERMINATE" at end? I'll decide no, because we propose index optimization. But maybe the prompt says "If you find the SQL query is already well-optimized... include TERMINATE". We don't find that. So no.

Let's write final answer with moderate detail.5537 rows) via hash or nested loop. Filter by part type reduces to ~4,200 rows. Then join supplier and nation2. This seems much less work than scanning 60M lineitem. Why didn't optimizer choose? Because it estimated cost of 379k index scans (random I/O) plus 1.5M rows vs seq scan 60M. But if data is cached or index is fast, it might be better. The optimizer cost model might think seq scan is cheaper. However, forcing it with MATERIALIZED CTE could yield a better plan in practice. This is a SQL rewrite that could improve performance without indexes! But the previous report's corrected_guide_knowledge said "Avoid wrapping the query in a CTE: this query is a flat multi-way join with no repeated subquery computation to amortize, so a CTE provides no reuse benefit and (in older PostgreSQL versions) can force materialization, blocking predicate pushdown and join reordering. Keep the join tree inline so the optimizer can freely reorder joins and push the selective filters..." That advice is generic. But here we might intentionally use MATERIALIZED CTE to force a different join order because the optimizer is choosing a suboptimal plan? The explain shows cost 1.9M. Is it suboptimal? The plan scans lineitem full 60M. If we force starting from orders, we use index on l_orderkey. But is there an index on l_orderkey? In TPC-H, lineitem primary key is (l_orderkey, l_linenumber). So yes. The optimizer could use it, but chose seq scan. Why? Because 379k orders * index lookup is expensive. Let's calculate: 379k index probes, each returns ~4 rows. Random I/O 379k. Seq scan of 60M rows is sequential. On modern hardware, 60M rows seq scan might be faster than 379k random I/Os. But if lineitem is clustered by l_orderkey? TPC-H lineitem is not clustered by orderkey; it's generated by order? Actually TPC-H lineitem is ordered by l_orderkey? In dbgen, lineitem is generated per order, so it is clustered by l_orderkey? The primary key index is on l_orderkey, but the table might be physically ordered by l_orderkey. If so, index scan might be efficient. But the optimizer chose seq scan, so its cost model thinks seq scan is better. Forcing may or may not help. The previous report's guide says avoid CTE. So probably not recommended.

Another SQL rewrite: Use a subquery to pre-filter lineitem by part type using EXISTS? No.

Given the previous report and the guide knowledge, the recommended strategy is to add indexes, not CTE. The SQL rewrite to explicit joins and FILTER is already done. Since the previous attempt didn't improve plan, the next step is indexes. The question is whether to output the same SQL and say "with indexes, this SQL is optimal". I think that's appropriate.

</analysis>
The rewritten SQL is TPC-H Q8: it computes CANADA's market share within AMERICA for orders in 1995–1996 for 'ECONOMY PLATED STEEL' parts. Rewriting steps: (1) inlined the derived table and converted implicit joins to explicit ANSI JOINs; (2) replaced SUM(CASE...) with SUM(... FILTER ...) + COALESCE, preserving equivalence including the 0 result for years with no Canadian sales; (3) kept all selective filters in the main WHERE so the optimizer can push them down. Based on data statistics (lineitem 60M rows, part 2M rows, filtered part ≈ 5.5K rows yielding ≈ 166K lineitem rows), the dominant cost is the full lineitem scan; a covering index on lineitem(l_partkey) combined with an index on part(p_type) would let the plan start from ~5.5K parts and probe lineitem (~166K rows) instead of scanning 60M rows. No CTE is created because this flat join has no redundant subquery computation and a materialized CTE would risk blocking predicate pushdown and join reordering. Given the previous report confirmed that this SQL text rewrite produces an identical plan, no further SQL-text-only rewrite can improve performance; the remaining gain requires the physical indexes above. The query is therefore already in its optimal SQL form.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
SELECT
    EXTRACT(YEAR FROM o.o_orderdate) AS o_year,
    COALESCE(
        SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount))
            FILTER (WHERE n2.n_name = 'CANADA'),
        0
    ) / SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS mkt_share
FROM part p
JOIN lineitem l ON l.l_partkey = p.p_partkey
JOIN supplier s ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
JOIN orders o ON o.o_orderkey = l.l_orderkey
JOIN customer c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
JOIN nation n1 ON n1.n_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
JOIN region r ON r.r_regionkey = n1.n_regionkey
JOIN nation n2 ON n2.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
WHERE p.p_type = 'ECONOMY PLATED STEEL'
  AND o.o_orderdate BETWEEN DATE '1995-01-01' AND DATE '1996-12-31'
  AND r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
GROUP BY EXTRACT(YEAR FROM o.o_orderdate)
ORDER BY o_year;
```

A careful line-by-line comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs reveals that all three plans are structurally and numerically identical:

1. **Original vs. Rewritten**: The rewritten query only replaces full table names with short aliases (e.g., `supplier` → `s`, `lineitem` → `l`, `orders` → `o`, `customer` → `c`, `region` → `r`). Every cost value, row estimate, width, node type, join condition, filter, and worker count is byte-for-byte identical. Aliasing alone has no effect on PostgreSQL's optimizer, so the identical plans are the expected and correct outcome.

2. **Rewritten vs. Enhanced**: The enhanced EXPLAIN output is also completely identical to the rewritten one. No rewrite rule, index suggestion, join reorder, or predicate transformation materially changed the plan chosen by the optimizer. This suggests that either (a) the applied enhancements were semantically neutral and the optimizer had already converged on the optimal plan, or (b) no applicable physical optimization (e.g., new indexes) took effect for this plan shape.

Key observations supporting this conclusion:
- Total plan cost is 1917045.25..1917965.35 in all three plans (0.00% change).
- The most expensive node — the Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem feeding the part join (cost up to ~1,492,167.85, and the overall Parallel Hash Join reaching 1,911,730.41) — is unchanged.
- Scan types are identical throughout: Parallel Seq Scans on lineitem, orders, customer, supplier, part; plain Seq Scans on nation and region; no index scans anywhere.
- Join algorithms are identical: a tree of Parallel Hash Joins and Hash Joins, with no transition to Merge Join or Nested Loop.
- The explicit Sort node before Partial GroupAggregate persists in all plans (cost 1916045.24..1916080.65), so no sort elimination occurred.
- Workers Planned remains 1 in the Gather Merge node; the degree of parallelism is unchanged.
- Row estimates and widths are identical at every node, indicating no improvement in cardinality estimation or projection pruning.

1. **Cost Efficiency**
   - Overall cost change: **0.00%**. The total plan cost remains 1,917,045.25 (startup) to 1,917,965.35 (total) across all three plans. The rewrite and enhancement steps did not reduce the estimated execution cost.
   - Most expensive node: The dominant cost contributor — the Parallel Hash Join combining lineitem with the part-filtered hash build (cost ≈ 1,911,730.41, driven by the full Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem at ≈ 1,375,081.30) — shows **no cost reduction**. This node processes ~25M lineitem rows and remains the primary optimization target for future work.

2. **Plan Characteristics**
   - Scan type transitions: **None**. All three plans rely exclusively on Sequential Scans (parallelized for the large tables: lineitem, orders, customer, supplier, part). The date-range filter on orders (1995-01-01 to 1996-12-31) and the p_type filter on part are strong candidates for B-tree / composite indexes that would enable Index or Bitmap Index Scans, but none were introduced.
   - Join algorithm refinements: **None**. The plan retains the same hash-based join tree (Hash Join + Parallel Hash Join) in all versions. Given the small nation/region dimension tables and large fact-table scans, hash joins are reasonable here, so the optimizer's choice is defensible even though no refinement was realized.
   - Sorting and intermediate results: The explicit **Sort node persists** (cost ≈ 1,917,080) ahead of the Partial GroupAggregate on EXTRACT(year FROM o_orderdate). No sort elimination or reduction of intermediate result sets was achieved; row estimates (e.g., 14,161 rows into the sort, 2,406 groups out) are identical in all plans.

3. **Resource Utilization**
   - Memory: Hash build sizes are unchanged — the largest hash builds remain the lineitem-derived Parallel Hash (~100,95 rows estimate at the supplier join level) and the orders join hash (~166,071 rows). Multiple Parallel Hash nodes still imply significant shared workfile/hash memory consumption per worker; no reduction was achieved.
   - Workers: **Workers Planned: 1** in all plans. No adjustment to parallel degree was made. For a plan dominated by a 25M-row scan on lineitem, a higher max_parallel_workers_per_gather could meaningfully reduce wall-clock time, but this was not reflected in the enhanced plan.

4. **Other Improvements**
   - Readability: The sole tangible improvement is **query readability/maintainability** — short table aliases (o, l, s, c, p, r, n1, n2) make the plan output and SQL easier to read, with zero impact on semantics or performance.
   - Estimation quality: Row estimates and widths are identical across plans; no improvement in cardinality estimation or column projection pruning was observed.
   - Recommendation: Since the plan is cost-dominated by the full scan of lineitem and the post-join Sort, future optimization should prioritize (a) indexes supporting the orders date-range predicate and the part p_type filter, (b) increasing parallelism for the lineitem scan, and (c) investigating an index on EXTRACT(year FROM o_orderdate) or partitioning orders/lineitem by year to eliminate the explicit Sort.

Support this with covering indexes: `part(p_type) INCLUDE (p_partkey)` and `lineitem(l_partkey) INCLUDE (l_orderkey, l_suppkey, l_extendedprice, l_discount)`, replacing the 60M-row full scan with an index probe per matching part."
    },
    {
        "group": "Query_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Keep the date-range predicate in sargable form (direct literal bounds DATE '1995-01-01' / DATE '1996-12-31' on o.o_orderdate, already in place) so the planner can use the 2-year filter. Only create the optional covering index `orders(o_orderdate) INCLUDE (o_orderkey, o_custkey)` if execution-plan validation shows the date filter is actually being used to drive/limit the scan; otherwise the part->lineitem (p_type) index path is sufficient. Validate with fresh statistics on p.p_type, o.o_orderdate and r.r_name, comparing the p_type-driven plan against the o_orderdate-driven plan, and materialize the orders index only when the statistics show the date predicate is selective enough to win."
    },
    {
        "group": "Constant_folding",
        "produced_suggestion": "No further constant folding needed: the literals (p_type = 'ECONOMY PLATED STEEL', r.r_name = 'AMERICA', the n2.n_name = 'CANADA' predicate, and the o_orderdate range) are already plain constant expressions pushed down to the base table scans. Keep these predicates as raw literals — do not wrap them in functions or computations that would prevent constant folding. Potential final SQL:

```sql
SELECT
    EXTRACT(YEAR FROM o.o_orderdate) AS o_year,
    COALESCE(
        SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount))
            FILTER (WHERE n2.n_name = 'CANADA'),
        0
    ) / SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS mkt_share
FROM part p
JOIN lineitem l ON l.l_partkey = p.p_partkey
JOIN supplier s ON s.s_s

=== Worker 0: Start Rewrite ===
uppkey = l.l_suppkey
JOIN orders o ON o.o_orderkey = l.l_orderkey
JOIN customer c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
JOIN nation n1 ON n1.n_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
JOIN region r ON r.r_regionkey = n1.n_regionkey
JOIN nation n2 ON n2.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
WHERE p.p_type = 'ECONOMY PLATED STEEL'
  AND o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1995-01-01'
  AND o.o_orderdate < DATE '1997-01-01'
  AND r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
GROUP BY EXTRACT(YEAR FROM o.o_orderdate)
ORDER BY o_year;
```

Is this equivalent to `BETWEEN '1995-01-01' AND '1996-12-31'`? Yes, for DATE type. If o_orderdate includes time (but it's date), `< '1997-01-01'` is equivalent. Good.

CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS idx_lineitem_l_partkey_covering
  ON lineitem (l_partkey) INCLUDE (l_orderkey, l_suppkey, l_extendedprice, l_discount);

-- Optional, only if EXPLAIN shows a date-driven plan is better:

  ON orders (o_orderdate) INCLUDE (o_orderkey, o_custkey);

ANALYZE lineitem;
ANALYZE orders;
ANALYZE customer;

The flattened, FILTER-based rewrite is logically optimal: the previous report confirmed the execution plan is identical to the original, so no further SQL-level rewriting (CTEs, subquery restructuring, predicate changes) will improve the plan. The real bottleneck is physical: the plan hash-joins the entire lineitem table (60M rows) against only ~5.5K parts matching p_type (~0.28% selectivity), while the o_orderdate filter (12.7%) is far less selective. Creating a covering index on lineitem(l_partkey) lets the planner drive a nested-loop join from the selective part filter, reducing the lineitem access from 60M rows to roughly 166K rows (5

SQL-only rewrites cannot fix this; the most efficient optimization is physical: create a covering index on part(p_type) INCLUDE (p_partkey) and lineitem(l_partkey) INCLUDE (l_orderkey, l_suppkey, l_extendedprice, l_discount), then ANALYZE, to enable a part-first nested-loop join that avoids scanning all of lineitem."

</advice>

</analysis>
The original SQL is TPC-H Q8. The derived table is already flattened by the optimizer, and the previous rewrite (ANSI joins + FILTER) produced an identical plan, so SQL-text-only changes are performance-neutral here. The bottleneck is the full Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem (~60M rows) even though the `p_type = 'ECONOMY PLATED STEEL'` filter is extremely selective (~5.5K parts out of 2M). The most effective optimization is a part-first index-driven join path. The rewritten SQL below is semantically equivalent (COALESCE preserves the original ELSE 0 numerator behavior), cleaner, and uses a sargable date range predicate. It should be paired with the covering indexes and fresh statistics so the planner chooses a nested-loop plan starting from the filtered part rows instead of scanning lineitem.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
SELECT
    EXTRACT(YEAR FROM o.o_orderdate) AS o_year,
    COALESCE(
        SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount))
            FILTER (WHERE n2.n_name = 'CANADA'),
        0
    ) / SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS mkt_share
FROM part p
JOIN lineitem l ON l.l_partkey = p.p_partkey
JOIN supplier s ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
JOIN orders o ON o.o_orderkey = l.l_orderkey
JOIN customer c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
JOIN nation n1 ON n1.n_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
JOIN region r ON r.r_regionkey = n1.n_regionkey
JOIN nation n2 ON n2.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
WHERE p.p_type = 'ECONOMY PLATED STEEL'
  AND o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1995-01-01'
  AND o.o_orderdate < DATE '1997-01-01'
  AND r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
GROUP BY EXTRACT(YEAR FROM o.o_orderdate)
ORDER BY o_year;
```

</analysis>
Both statements correctly flatten the derived-table into explicit JOINs and use FILTER for the conditional sum, producing the same result. The difference is the date predicate: statement 1 uses a half-open range (o_orderdate >= '1995-01-01' AND o_orderdate < '1997-01-01'), which is the best practice for date/timestamp columns — it is fully sargable regardless of whether o_orderdate is DATE or TIMESTAMP, and it cleanly includes all of 1996 without relying on inclusive BETWEEN semantics. Statement 0's BETWEEN works for DATE but is more fragile if o_orderdate is a timestamp with a time component. Statement 1 is therefore the most effective rewrite.
</analysis>
